description: "How Klive turns the ZX Spectrum Next's sound into what your computer plays: one sample every 583 master clocks with the fraction carried across frames, averaging a sample's window against point sampling and the aliasing it prevents, the beeper's DC filter, a frame's samples on their way to the sound card, the queue that absorbs the jitter, what you hear when the machine stops, steps or replays, how the sound is tested, and the whole sound system in one picture."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# From the Machine to Your Speakers: Sound in an Emulator

Here is a puzzle for you. The Next's sound can change on any of 28 million clock ticks a second. Your
computer's sound card wants 48,000 numbers a second, evenly spaced, delivered on time, every time. And the
emulator in between runs the machine in bursts: it races through a whole frame in a few milliseconds, then
sits idle until the next one is due, and now and then it stops dead at a breakpoint for as long as you like.
So how does anything smooth ever come out of the speakers?

You hear the answer every time you run a program in Klive, and you notice it only when it goes wrong. A
sound that clicks at every frame, a tone that rattles with notes nobody wrote, a delay between a key press
and its beep, an echo of old sound after the debugger stops: each of these is a decision made somewhere on
the way from the machine to your ears. This last sound chapter is about those decisions.

Everything before was the Next. This chapter is Klive. It builds on
[The Mixer: One Sum, Three Outputs](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/), whose How Klive
does it explains how Klive adds the sources and which output it plays, and on the background chapter
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) for samples, sample rates
and aliasing.

## The road ahead

We start with the two clocks that never agree, the machine's and the sound card's, and the small piece of
arithmetic that keeps them in step across every frame. Then we look at what a sample actually is: a moment, or
a window? That choice decides whether a high beeper note plays clean or grows notes of its own, and I will show
you both. A short stop at the beeper's DC filter comes next. Then the samples travel: out of the core, across to
the audio thread, into a queue, and on to the sound card. The queue is the hero of the second half, the part I
would ask you to remember, because it is what turns a machine that runs in bursts into sound that flows. We
see what it does when the machine stops, steps or replays, how Klive tests its own sound, and we end with the
whole sound system in one picture.

<Callout type="info">
The numbers are for the Next's **28 MHz master clock**, **128K timing** (a frame of 456 × 311 positions, 20.26 ms)
and a sound card at **48,000 samples a second**. Klive plays at your sound card's own rate, so yours may be 44,100
or another; the arithmetic is the same. The counts and the spectra are captured from Klive's emulated Next; the
queue is Klive's audio code run on a timeline.
</Callout>

## Two clocks that never agree

A sample every 28,000,000 ÷ 48,000 = **583.33** master clocks. A frame is 456 × 311 positions of the 7 MHz
dot clock, four master clocks each: **567,264** clocks. Divide one by the other and a frame holds
**972.45 samples**. Not 972, not 973. The sound card does not care about frames, of course; it wants its 48,000
a second whatever the machine is doing.

So Klive keeps the time of the next sample in master clocks, multiplied by the sample rate so that it stays a
whole number, and it never starts that count afresh. When a frame ends, the schedule moves back by a
frame's length and carries on. The next frame's first sample comes 583.33 clocks after the previous frame's last
one, wherever that falls:

<figure>

Bottom, the samples each of 40 frames carried, captured from the machine: 972 or 973, mostly 972, with a 973 about every second frame, averaging 972.45." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:carry */}
<figcaption>Samples across a frame's end. The schedule runs on, so the first sample of the next frame keeps its distance from the last one, and each frame carries 972 or 973 samples, 972.45 on average.</figcaption>
</figure>

Why does that fraction matter so much? Well, suppose each frame restarted its samples at the frame's first
clock. Then every frame would throw away its 0.45 of a sample: 22 samples a second, gone. The sound would play
a little slow, the queue would slowly run dry, and at every frame's end two samples would sit a fraction too
far apart, a tiny click 49 times a second. With the fraction carried, every run of frames holds within one
sample of the exact total, as the capture shows.

## A moment or a window?

The next question sounds philosophical, but it is not. When Klive takes a sample, should it take the level **at
that moment**, or the **average over the window** since the last sample?

For the DACs and the Pi it hardly matters: their level changes only when a new value arrives, and a sample takes
the value standing at its moment. For the beeper and the AY chips it matters a lot. A beeper edge or an AY tone
edge can fall anywhere between two samples, and here is what each choice makes of it:

<figure>
{/* fig:average */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 290" role="img" aria-label="A beeper square wave whose edges fall anywhere between the sample moments. Top, the wave itself, with the sample windows marked by grid lines. Middle, point sampling: each sample takes the wave's level at its moment, so the steps are whole samples long and an edge jumps to the next sample. Bottom, averaging the window: each sample takes the share of its window the wave spent high, so a sample that an edge cuts lands between the two levels." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="0" y="234" fill="#e5484d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">averaged</text>
  <text x="0" y="252" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">jack, speaker</text>
  <text x="140" y="288" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">grid: one sample window, 1/48,000 s</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:average */}
<figcaption>A 9,358 Hz beeper tone, sampled two ways. Point sampling moves each edge to the next sample; averaging the window gives a sample that an edge cuts the share of the window the wave spent high.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Point sampling** moves every edge to the next sample, up to 21 µs late. **Averaging** keeps each edge's
position in the size of the sample it cuts: an edge a third of the way into a window gives that sample two thirds
of the high level. Klive averages the beeper's EAR and MIC bits over each window, weighted by the time they spent
high, and the AY chips' outputs over the chip ticks in the window. That is the default, for the jack and the
speaker. For HDMI it point-samples, because the Next's HDMI audio really does take the sum's value at each of its
48,000 moments, as the mixer chapter's HDMI audio explains.

You can hear the difference. A square wave is not one tone: it has harmonics at three, five, seven times its
pitch, and a 9,358 Hz tone has its fifth harmonic at 46,791 Hz, far above what 48,000 samples a second can hold.
Point sampling folds it back down, to 48,000 − 46,791 = **1,209 Hz**, a low growl the program never asked for.
Here are the two captured:

<figure>
{/* fig:spectrum */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 360" role="img" aria-label="Two spectra of the same captured beeper tone, from 0 to 24 kHz, the loudness of each frequency in decibels below the tone, down to 60. Point sampling, as HDMI does: the tone at 9.36 kHz, and an alias at 1.21 kHz about 14 decibels below it, plus another at 19.9 kHz and smaller ones. Averaging the window, as the jack does: the tone, nothing at 1.21 kHz, and the alias at 19.9 kHz still there, a few decibels weaker." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The same beeper tone captured through HDMI (point sampling) and the jack (averaging), as spectra. Point sampling adds an alias at 1.2 kHz; averaging all but removes it. The alias at 19.9 kHz, the third harmonic folded back, survives both.</figcaption>
</figure>

Averaging takes the 1,209 Hz alias down to half a per cent of the tone. But let me warn you not to think of it as a
perfect filter. An average over one window is a rough one: it nearly removes what lies close to 48,000 Hz and its
multiples, and much less of what lies between. The third harmonic, 28,075 Hz, folds back to 19,925 Hz, and the
average only halves it. Luckily that one sits at the very top of human hearing, where most of us hear nothing at
all. A truly clean result needs a real low-pass filter before sampling, which costs far more than an average, and
the beeper rarely plays that high. Klive chose the average: cheap, exact in its timing, and good where it counts.

## The beeper's DC filter

One more thing happens to the beeper before it reaches the mix. The Next adds only levels from 0 upwards, so a
beeper that is switched on adds 512 units and stays there; the capacitor after the hardware's converters removes
that constant level before your speaker ever sees it. Klive does the same job with a **high-pass filter at
1.4 Hz** on EAR and MIC: far below any pitch, so every note passes untouched, but a level that stays put drifts
back to zero over a few tenths of a second. The mixer chapter's How Klive does it shows the beeper switched on
from silence before and after the capacitor; Klive's samples are the "after".

## From a frame to the sound card

The core collects a frame's samples, left and right, as the frame runs. When the frame ends, they set off:

The renderer copies them into one block of floating-point numbers and passes it to the audio thread without copying. The worklet keeps a ring of eight frames and holds three back before it plays. </svg>
{/* /fig:pipeline */}
<figcaption>The way out. A frame's samples leave the core when the frame ends, travel to the audio thread in one block, wait in the worklet's queue, and are pulled by the sound card at its own pace.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. The emulator's thread takes the frame's 972 or 973 sample pairs from the core.
2. The renderer copies them into one block of floating-point numbers, scaled by your volume setting, and hands
   the block to the browser's audio thread without copying it again. That detail matters more than it looks: a
   plain list of 1,900 numbers every frame made garbage the browser had to clean up, and the clean-up could stall
   the frame loop long enough to starve the sound.
3. On the audio thread, an **audio worklet**, a small piece of code the browser runs right next to the sound card,
   puts the block in its queue.
4. The sound card pulls samples from the worklet in callbacks of 512, about every 10.7 ms at 48,000 a second (as
   measured on a Mac), whatever the emulator is doing at that moment.

## A queue against the jitter

Now look at the timing. Frames arrive every 20.26 ms, but never exactly then: the emulator's timer, the browser
and the rest of your computer push each one a few milliseconds earlier or later. The sound card, meanwhile, pulls
every 10.7 ms like clockwork. If the queue holds only what the last frame brought, sooner or later the card asks
for samples that have not arrived yet. So the worklet keeps a cushion:

- It holds a ring of **eight frames'** worth of samples.
- Before it plays, at the start and after it has run dry, it **holds back three frames**: about 60 ms between the
  machine and your ears. Why three? With one, the queue fell to a few milliseconds just before each frame
  arrived; with two, it still ran dry now and then, and every dry spell faded out and back in, a click every few
  seconds. Three keep a good 25 ms in hand at the lowest point, even when frames arrive 10 ms late.
- A **lone frame** is the exception: one frame and then nothing, as when the debugger steps the machine. Once two
  frames' worth of time has passed with nothing new, the worklet plays what it has instead of waiting for ever.
  Two, not one, because the card asks every 10.7 ms, so two callbacks, more than a frame's time, often pass
  between two frames of a machine that is keeping up perfectly well.
- If it ever holds more than **five frames**, because frames came in faster than they were played, it drops the
  oldest. Better to skip a little than to let the delay grow.
- When it runs dry, it does not cut to zero, which would click. It **fades** the last level out over a few
  milliseconds, and when sound arrives again it **fades in** over about 2 ms.

Here is the queue at work, its rules run over 1.2 seconds, with a stretch in the middle where the machine
delivers a frame only every 30 ms, as a busy debugging session can:

<figure>
{/* fig:queue */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 330" role="img" aria-label="A plot over 1.2 seconds of how much sound, in milliseconds, waits in Klive's audio queue. At the start the queue fills for three frames before anything plays, about 64 milliseconds. Then it rises by a frame, about 20 milliseconds, at each arrival and drains steadily between them, between about 34 and 53 milliseconds. From 0.4 to 0.76 seconds the machine delivers a frame only every 30 milliseconds: the queue drains, runs dry, and the sound stops in short gaps until a frame arrives. Back in step, it refills and plays on. A strip under the plot marks when sound plays." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>How much sound waits in the queue, by the worklet's own rules. In step with real time it rides between about 34 and 53 ms; when frames come too slowly it drains, runs dry and plays in gaps, then refills once the machine is back in step.</figcaption>
</figure>

Notice what the slow stretch sounds like: gaps, not a stutter. Once the queue is dry, the worklet waits again, as it did at the start, before it plays, so you hear short pieces of correct sound with silence in between,
rather than a sound that stops and starts with every sample. Hey, if your program sounds choppy only while the
debugger is busy, now you know why.

## When the machine stops

The emulator does more than run. Here is what you hear in each of its other moods:

- **Paused or stopped**, at a breakpoint or by your hand: Klive suspends the sound card's audio and empties the
  queue. Nothing old is left to play when you go on, so you never hear an echo of the moment before the stop.
- **Running under the debugger**: while the machine keeps up with real time, you hear the sound as usual. When it
  falls behind, as when the debugger runs it an instruction at a time, or it waits for the SD card, you hear the
  sound in gaps.
- **Stepping**: only a finished frame sends sound, and a single step rarely finishes one, so stepping is silent.
  A lone frame that arrives with nothing after it is not kept waiting for ever: once two frames' worth of time
  has passed, the worklet plays what it has.
- **Reverse debugging and replays**: to show you an earlier moment, Klive runs the machine again from a saved
  state. Those frames reach neither the screen nor the speakers. They are history being retraced at whatever speed
  the computer can manage, and playing them would be sound from the past at the wrong pace.
- **Rendering a recording to a video file**: the machine runs as fast as it can, so the live sound is muted, and
  every sample goes to the recording instead, which plays it later at the right speed.

## How Klive does it

Most of this chapter has been Klive already, so this section is short: where each piece lives, and how Klive
makes sure its sound is right.

The sound is tested like the rest of the machine. The Next's test harness runs the real core and records the
samples of every completed frame, exactly as the IDE gets them. Tests then check what can be measured: a tone's
pitch from its edges, a level's size in mixer units, which side a channel lands on, the samples each frame
carries. Every waveform in the sound chapters comes from such a test, which first checks the facts its chapter
states and only then writes the capture the figure is drawn from. The worklet has tests of its own for its queue. If you want to read the code yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The sample schedule, averaging, the DC filter, the outputs | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-audio-mixer.c` |
| The beeper's time-weighted EAR and MIC | `zxnext-beeper.c` in the same folder |
| The AY chips' average over a sample's window | `zxnext-psg.c` in the same folder |
| A frame's samples to the renderer and the recorder | `src/renderer/features/emulator/audioFrameRendering.ts` |
| The audio context and the block handed to the audio thread | `src/renderer/features/emulator/AudioRenderer.ts` |
| The queue: hold-back, trimming, fading | `src/renderer/features/emulator/Sampling.worklet.js` |
| Frames paced to real time, and what a replay sends | `src/emu/machines/MachineController.ts` |
| The scenes behind the sound chapters' figures | `test/zxnext-hw/audio/next-audio-scene.test.ts` |

## What to take home

So here is the whole sound system, from the first chapter to this one:

<svg viewBox="0 0 700 380" role="img" aria-label="The whole sound system of the ZX Spectrum Next as the sound chapters told it. On the left, the sources: the beeper with EAR, MIC and the tape, three AY chips behind TurboSound, four 8-bit DACs and the Raspberry Pi's audio. They all go into one mixer with a left and a right side. The mixer's sums go to the audio jack, the internal speaker, HDMI and back to the Pi. {/* /fig:map */}
<figcaption>The Next's sound system as the sound chapters told it, and the last step, Klive's.</figcaption>
</figure>

On the Next, four kinds of source make sound. The **beeper** is two bits of port `$FE`, with the tape on the same
line. Three **AY chips** play tones, noise and envelopes by themselves, and TurboSound puts all three behind the
128K's two ports. Four **8-bit DACs** play whatever numbers a program, the CTC, the DMA or the Copper writes, on
the ports of six old sound cards. The **Raspberry Pi** streams samples over I2S, and can even play a tape into the
Next's EAR input. One **mixer** adds them all, a left and a right side, and its sums leave through the audio jack,
the internal speaker, HDMI and back to the Pi.

Klive plays one of those outputs. It takes a sample every 583.33 master clocks and carries the fraction from frame
to frame, so a frame holds 972 or 973 samples and none is ever lost. It averages the beeper and the AY chips over
each sample's window, which keeps a high beeper note from growling, and point-samples HDMI as the real one does.
Each finished frame's samples travel to an audio worklet, whose queue holds three frames back, about 60 ms, rides
about 34 to 53 ms ahead of your ears, trims beyond five frames, and fades rather than clicks when it runs dry. Stop the machine and the queue empties; replay history
and nothing plays.

And that is the whole journey, from a bit flipped by a Z80 to the cone of your speaker. 28:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Why does that fraction matter so much? Well, suppose each frame restarted its samples at the frame’s first\nclock. Then every frame would throw away its 0.45 of a sample: 22 samples a second, gone. The sound would play\na little slow, the queue would slowly run dry, and at every frame’s end two samples would sit a fraction too\nfar apart, a tiny click 49 times a second. With the fraction carried, every run of frames holds within one\nsample of the exact total, as the capture shows."}]

2d:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Point sampling"}]," moves every edge to the next sample, up to 21 µs late. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Averaging"}]," keeps each edge’s\nposition in the size of the sample it cuts: an edge a third of the way into a window gives that sample two thirds\nof the high level. Klive averages the beeper’s EAR and MIC bits over each window, weighted by the time they spent\nhigh, and the AY chips’ outputs over the chip ticks in the window. That is the default, for the jack and the\nspeaker. For HDMI it point-samples, because the Next’s HDMI audio really does take the sum’s value at each of its\n48,000 moments, as the mixer chapter’s HDMI audio explains."]}]

30:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Averaging takes the 1,209 Hz alias down to half a per cent of the tone. But let me warn you not to think of it as a\nperfect filter. An average over one window is a rough one: it nearly removes what lies close to 48,000 Hz and its\nmultiples, and much less of what lies between. The third harmonic, 28,075 Hz, folds back to 19,925 Hz, and the\naverage only halves it. Luckily that one sits at the very top of human hearing, where most of us hear nothing at\nall. A truly clean result needs a real low-pass filter before sampling, which costs far more than an average, and\nthe beeper rarely plays that high. Klive chose the average: cheap, exact in its timing, and good where it counts."}]

32:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["One more thing happens to the beeper before it reaches the mix. The Next adds only levels from 0 upwards, so a\nbeeper that is switched on adds 512 units and stays there; the capacitor after the hardware’s converters removes\nthat constant level before your speaker ever sees it. Klive does the same job with a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"high-pass filter at\n1.4 Hz"}]," on EAR and MIC: far below any pitch, so every note passes untouched, but a level that stays put drifts\nback to zero over a few tenths of a second. The mixer chapter’s How Klive does it shows the beeper switched on\nfrom silence before and after the capacitor; Klive’s samples are the “after”."]}]

38:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Now look at the timing. Frames arrive every 20.26 ms, but never exactly then: the emulator’s timer, the browser\nand the rest of your computer push each one a few milliseconds earlier or later. The sound card, meanwhile, pulls\nevery 10.7 ms like clockwork. If the queue holds only what the last frame brought, sooner or later the card asks\nfor samples that have not arrived yet. So the worklet keeps a cushion:"}]
39:["$","ul",null,{"className":"x:[:is(ol,ul)_&]:my-[.75em] x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:list-disc x:ms-[1.5em]","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["It holds a ring of ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"eight frames’"}]," worth of samples."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["Before it plays, at the start and after it has run dry, it ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"holds back three frames"}],": about 60 ms between the\nmachine and your ears. Why three? With one, the queue fell to a few milliseconds just before each frame\narrived; with two, it still ran dry now and then, and every dry spell faded out and back in, a click every few\nseconds. Three keep a good 25 ms in hand at the lowest point, even when frames arrive 10 ms late."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["A ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"lone frame"}]," is the exception: one frame and then nothing, as when the debugger steps the machine. Once two\nframes’ worth of time has passed with nothing new, the worklet plays what it has instead of waiting for ever.\nTwo, not one, because the card asks every 10.7 ms, so two callbacks, more than a frame’s time, often pass\nbetween two frames of a machine that is keeping up perfectly well."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["If it ever holds more than ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"five frames"}],", because frames came in faster than they were played, it drops the\noldest. Better to skip a little than to let the delay grow."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"className":"x:my-[.5em]","children":["When it runs dry, it does not cut to zero, which would click. It ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"fades"}]," the last level out over a few\nmilliseconds, and when sound arrives again it ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"fades in"}]," over about 2 ms."]}],"\n"]}]

42:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"The sound is tested like the rest of the machine. The Next’s test harness runs the real core and records the\nsamples of every completed frame, exactly as the IDE gets them. Tests then check what can be measured: a tone’s\npitch from its edges, a level’s size in mixer units, which side a channel lands on, the samples each frame\ncarries. Every waveform in the sound chapters comes from such a test, which first checks the facts its chapter\nstates and only then writes the capture the figure is drawn from. 47:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["On the Next, four kinds of source make sound. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"beeper"}]," is two bits of port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}],", with the tape on the same\nline. Three ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"AY chips"}]," play tones, noise and envelopes by themselves, and TurboSound puts all three behind the\n128K’s two ports. Four ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"8-bit DACs"}]," play whatever numbers a program, the CTC, the DMA or the Copper writes, on\nthe ports of six old sound cards. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Raspberry Pi"}]," streams samples over I2S, and can even play a tape into the\nNext’s EAR input. 48:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Klive plays one of those outputs. It takes a sample every 583.33 master clocks and carries the fraction from frame\nto frame, so a frame holds 972 or 973 samples and none is ever lost. It averages the beeper and the AY chips over\neach sample’s window, which keeps a high beeper note from growling, and point-samples HDMI as the real one does.\nEach finished frame’s samples travel to an audio worklet, whose queue holds three frames back, about 60 ms, rides\nabout 34 to 53 ms ahead of your ears, trims beyond five frames, and fades rather than clicks when it runs dry. Stop the machine and the queue empties; replay history\nand nothing plays."}]
